People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Not constantly, exactly, but this whole up-and-down thing with my mood and energy started around when the problems with the new district manager really began. I guess that’s when I first noticed it getting harder.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself losing interest or not getting pleasure from things you usually enjoy, like hobbies, work, or time with friends and family?\n\nPatient: A little, yes. I mean, I still try to do the things I usually like… like I’ll set out my paints, or I’ll think, “I should go to pottery class, that’ll help.” But then when I’m there, I sort of just sit and stare at the materials. It doesn’t feel bad exactly, just… flat. Like the spark I used to get from it isn’t really there. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a big drop in your energy level or found yourself feeling more tired than usual, even when you're doing regular activities?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I’m not collapsing or anything, but even if I’ve been in bed all night, I’ll still feel worn out pretty quickly. Like opening the store, answering just a few staff questions, or even carrying groceries in from the car can feel like more than it should. Maybe that sounds minor, but it’s more than I’m used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight recently—like eating much less or much more than usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in any big way. My appetite’s been… pretty normal, I guess. Maybe some days I don’t think much about food until later, but I haven’t noticed my clothes fitting differently or anything like that. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling more guilty or like you're not good enough for things lately?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little… I don’t want to make it sound dramatic. But I do catch myself going over small things at work, like if a display sign was crooked or one of the trainees looked confused after I explained something. And then I’ll think, “Well, what kind of supervisor am I if I can’t even get that right?” It’s probably silly, but in the moment I can start feeling pretty useless.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself thinking more slowly than usual or moving more slowly, like your thoughts or body feel sluggish or weighed down?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I don’t know if I’d call it severe or anything. Some days I notice I’m kind of pacing around the living room and picking at my cuticles, like I can’t quite settle. And then other days it’s the opposite—just walking from the break room to the sales floor feels oddly slow, like my body is heavier than it should be. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this way before, with your mood and energy being so low for a period of time?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, not exactly like I’m falling apart or anything, but this low, kind of flat feeling has been around for… probably about six months now. Long enough that the things that usually help me reset, like my art class or my morning walks, just haven’t really been doing that anymore. I kept thinking it would pass, but it’s been lingering.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time before this one where you felt this low or down for more than two weeks and it interfered with your usual life or activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I’ve had rough patches before, like anyone, but I don’t know that I’d say it was clearly more than two weeks or that it really interfered in a major way. I may be forgetting something, but nothing is coming to mind the way this stretch has. Does that answer it okay?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
